# Trainer targeting and race placement research
Stage 1 · 19 November 2026 · Sporting Life historical results through 18 November 2026

## A. Executive summary

The retained results show recurring trainer placements. Haggas repeatedly sent Flat runners to Royal Ascot, York and Goodwood. Henderson returned to Cheltenham and Aintree. Mullins had substantial repeat participation at Cheltenham, Aintree, Punchestown and Galway. These are observations about where horses ran. The records cannot establish a trainer’s intention when entering them.

Haggas in valuable Flat handicaps and Mullins in the pooled festival handicaps both exceeded normalised final-SP win expectations in 2025 and 2026. Both lost at final SP in 2026. Mullins’s non-favourites in corroborated Grade 1 races had stronger A/E than his favourites in both years. Their positive untrimmed returns turned negative in both years after removing the two largest winning races.

Across 86 fixed placements and the specified market-rank cuts, the results support further behavioural research. Across the five rank cuts, no pattern met the reporting floor, exceeded A/E of 1 and retained positive ROI after removing its two largest winning races in both comparison years. This is historical discovery.

Joint meetings can cover multiple days. Race families use the existing AW, Flat Turf and Jump subtype classifiers against provider type, surface, course, going and explicit format cues. Other/unknown includes bumpers and unsupported types and provides history context where their results reconcile.

Ordinary and valuable handicaps require an identity-matched boolean handicap flag and published winner’s prize. The fixed valuable threshold is 25,000 in the recorded currency. Keep GBP and EUR separate in matching. The measure uses the nominal prize paid to the winner. Racecourse, date and provider meeting ID anchor named meetings. Numeric class comes from matching structured source and DB class. Grade or Group requires an explicit provider designation, corroborating Class 1 and known handicap status. This is a conservative label-plus-structure check, so Irish designations with missing numeric class can only be tracked separately when the designation is present. The source has no independent grade field. Novice is an explicit published descriptor with a structured handicap flag. Conditions uses the explicit descriptor, numeric class and a non-handicap flag. Broader conditions and exact level-weights eligibility cannot be inferred reliably from a name, observed weights or prize amount.

The race and ride identifiers, horse identifiers, final stages, positions, NR status and final prices were reconciled against retained provider result evidence. The 9 October repair appears as eight complete books and 91 starters in the export. The earlier missing Down Royal and Downpatrick identities remain relevant to population and prior history. Source stage evidence and out-of-corpus starts limit every observed-history count. [Integrity audit](racing-2025-2026-data-market-trainer-audit.md) · [Verified 9 October repair](racing-2026-10-09-flat-results-repair.md)

The index counts are retained links for each exact course/date window, excluding hidden and abandoned entries. Complete counts in the table are based on the same indexed IDs. Meeting days and races can be present while a particular trainer’s handicap subtype cohort remains too small to assess.

The usable market sample covers 33,188 races and 307,458 starters across the matched 2024–2026 windows. Annual race totals include all stored racing families and open records. The market sample covers AW, Turf, Chase and Hurdle and requires source-matched settlement and the entire final SP book. The named meeting inventory counts all retained race types, including bumpers.

The Punchestown Festival has 40 of 40 indexed races with reconcilable markets each year. Galway has 53 of 53 in 2024, 52 of 53 in 2025 and 54 of 54 in 2026. Two observed Mullins starts are excluded from Galway’s 2025 complete-book results. Numeric class is available for just 22 races at Punchestown and six at Galway in each year. The meeting and handicap comparisons have substantially better support than a comprehensive Irish Grade 1 comparison.

The previous audit identifies 171 absent races at Down Royal and Downpatrick on 24 dates in 2025, and 130 on 18 dates in 2026. The 2025 figure includes 26 December. Those absences can affect both observed placement and previous-run histories for Irish horses even when their later festival starts are present. Missing races add no starters, losses or expected wins. [Confirmed omissions by date and provider race ID](racing-2026-data-integrity-impact-and-correction-plan.md)

**Valuable handicaps.** Haggas went from 21 winners in 85 stakes in 2025 to 19 in 106 in 2026. Market A/E fell from 1.620 to 1.299 and ROI from +26.4% to −12.5%. Exact-field rivals returned A/E of 0.876 and 0.942. In Haggas’s own common-support samples, target versus other A/E was 1.800 versus 1.733 in 2025 and 1.413 versus 0.951 in 2026. The observed target average SP lengthened from about 8.0 to 10.6 decimal.

**Ordinary handicaps and preparation.** Ordinary handicap A/E fell from 1.344 to 0.758. The lightly raced proxy fell from 1.083 to 0.872. Class-rise handicap runners fell from 1.697 to 0.947, and those dropping in class fell from 1.009 to 0.922. Handicap runners returning from 90 days or more fell from 1.340 to about 0.630. The observed valuable handicap population generally came off a shorter break, with a median of 28 days in 2025 and 33 in 2026.

**Meeting placement and class.** All matched Flat appearances at Royal Ascot were 15, 13 and 20 across 2024–2026, at York Ebor 16, 10 and 17, and at Goodwood 7, 17 and 15. Meeting handicap results remain small. The Group 1 stakes varied from six to 23 between the comparison years and the conditions-label stakes from seven to four. The Group 2/3, conditions and novice splits are in the appendix. They provide no stable market advantage.

Cheltenham Festival Chase/Hurdle starters totalled 16, 18 and 20 across the three matched years. Aintree totalled 12, 11 and nine. The pooled festival handicaps gave two winners from 23 stakes in 2025 and two from 21 in 2026. A/E was 1.495 and 1.248 on only 1.3 and 1.6 market-expected winners. ROI fell from +45.7% to −11.9%. Progress beyond two winning races cannot be assessed from these samples. Removing the two leaves a complete loss of remaining stakes each year.

Ordinary handicap A/E fell from 1.146 to 0.922. Grade 1 rose from 0.683 to 1.129 on 17 and 15 stakes, and Grade 2/3 stayed below 1. Henderson’s runners with corroborated recent graded form into a handicap gave one winner in each comparison year. Seasonal reappearances stayed below market expectation. Second runs after a qualifying break cleared A/E of 1 marginally in both years, with negative ROI. A third-run return in 2026 was concentrated in its largest winning races.

Matched Chase/Hurdle appearances across 2024–2026 totalled 67, 71 and 69 at Cheltenham, 26, 29 and 17 at Aintree, 78, 61 and 84 at Punchestown and 47, 38 and 45 at Galway. These recurring placements are well supported as counts. Festival handicap hurdle and chase subgroups have much smaller winner denominators. At Punchestown, handicap hurdle stakes increased from seven to 16 with one winner each year, and handicap chase stakes from 11 to 23 with two winners each. Galway went from zero winners in both subtypes to two in each. Cheltenham handicap hurdle winners went from two to zero. Aintree handicap chase returns were highly dependent on the top winning races.

Pooled festival handicap A/E was 0.959 in 2024, 1.774 in 2025 and 1.343 in 2026. The comparison years had 7/62 and 8/98 winners/stakes. Own matched A/E was higher for the target both years, but ROI moved from +50.0% to −28.6%. All-venue handicap hurdle A/E fell from 1.561 to 0.778 and handicap chase from 1.114 to 0.897. The recent graded-to-handicap placement also weakened. Festival timing and return sequences do not provide a uniformly successful preparation pattern.

The corroborated Grade 1 split had A/E of 1.182, 1.255 and 1.017 across 2024–2026. Its aggregate ROI was −20.2%, +66.3% and +26.3%. Removing the top two races made both recent years negative. This subset is tied to the documented coverage of class and Grade 1 labels. It cannot represent all Irish Grade 1 appearances.

**Gavin Cromwell, Punchestown.** For Chase and Hurdle at the course across all dates, the stable ran 57, 68 and 59 starters, with A/E of 1.077, 1.242 and 1.197. The common-support comparison favoured Punchestown in both recent years. ROI was −47.1%, −38.1% and −43.1%. His rank-one sample in 2026 was only three. This is a recurring course placement with a preliminary win-count difference relative to market and same-yard runners.

**Other examples.** Dan Skelton’s handicap runners after at least 90 days returned A/E of roughly 1.12, 1.05 and 1.20 across three seasons, with negative ROI over each. Tim Easterby repeatedly ran at Ripon and was just above 1 in the recent two years, with a negative ROI each year. Andrew Balding’s non-favourite lightly raced handicap proxy returned A/E of 1.030, 1.294 and 1.232. ROI went from +1.3% to +0.7% and −6.4%, and the recent years were negative after removing the two largest winning races.

Charlie Johnston’s non-favourite lightly raced handicap runners produced high raw ROI in both recent years. Removing two winning races made both years negative. His matched same-yard comparison also failed to favour the target in 2025. The bounded screen yielded repeat participation and a few repeat A/E directions, with wide variation in returns.

At a whole-field level A/E is mechanically one because the normalised market and fractional winner credits each sum to one. This table describes race composition and the cost of taking every runner at SP. The trainer comparisons use a runner-specific market expectation and the same-race rival control. Comparing identical grades across jurisdictions remains limited by the class requirement.

Haggas’s market-relative repeatability was clearer in the valuable handicap subgroup than in ordinary handicaps. Henderson’s two festival handicap winners per year are a small basis for comparing with his graded runners. Mullins showed the clearest crossover in pooled festival handicaps and in the strictly covered Grade 1 subset, while his ordinary handicap subtypes weakened. The gap between A/E and ROI is important in all three examples.

Class changes compare the immediate prior observed start at the same family and jurisdiction. OR changes use the same restriction. Distance moves use the immediate same-family start. Recent graded form and competitive graded form mean a corroborated graded race or a top-three finish in one of the prior three observed Chase/Hurdle starts, respectively. A negative indicator requires all available recent starts to be corroborated grades or class 2–7 races without a conflicting grade label. Otherwise the status stays unknown. A repeat course or festival appearance uses earlier starts at that venue or the same named meeting window. A provider race ID identifies an individual running, so these data do not certify a single race series across sponsor changes.

Henderson’s festival handicap median gap increased from 34 to 52 days, with earlier appearances at those meetings rising from six to 14. Mean observed OR stayed near 135 in both years. Mullins’s comparable gap stayed near 39 days, and prior same-event appearances rose from 22 to 37 as stakes increased from 62 to 98. Median earlier career starts rose from six to 9.5. The market saw a different mix of horses between years even under the same placement definition.

Mullins had 106 and 111 third runs after an observed 90-day break in 2025 and 2026, with A/E of 1.027 and 1.091 and ROI of −2.2% and +21.0%. The latter was −6.1% after removing its largest two winners. The 2024 spell counts are heavily affected by missing earlier seasons. These are repeatable descriptions of observed preparation. Both the uncovered dates and the availability of prior runs constrain conclusions about when the preparation actually began.

## I. Excluding-favourites results

Final SP competition rank equals one plus the number of shorter-priced starters. Equal prices share the rank. Two equal shortest SPs give ranks 1, 1, 3. All rank-one runners are favourites. Ranks 2–3 and 4+ are subsets of the non-favourite cut. Every included runner is one £1 win stake. No thresholds were optimised.

Cells show stakes/wins · market A/E · ROI. Stage 1 uses final rank as a retrospective cut. Which horses are favourites can change up to the off. These figures cannot establish a bet executable at a known pre-race time.

Excluding favourites weakened Haggas’s valuable handicap results. Favourite A/E was 1.937 and 1.565, against 1.279 and 0.949 among non-favourites. The non-favourite ROI moved from approximately +1% to −26%, alongside approximately +78% to +15% for favourites.

Mullins’s Grade 1 non-favourites improved on the favourite comparison in both recent years. From 119 and 86 stakes they supplied 15 and nine winners, A/E of 1.485 and 1.132, and ROI of +80.9% and +32.6%. The favourite A/E was 1.036 and 0.912, with negative ROI. The corresponding 2024 Grade 1 non-favourite ROI was −31.5%. The top-two removals returned −12.3% and −29.2% in the recent years. Mullins’s pooled festival handicap non-favourites had A/E above 1 in both recent years, but their ROI changed from about +52% to −34%.

Henderson’s Grade 1 non-favourite A/E was around 1.65 in both recent years from one winner in each and only six and eight stakes. The festival non-favourite A/E moved from 0.887 to 1.103 with one winner each. Balding’s lightly raced non-favourites held their A/E direction while favourite A/E fell sharply in 2026. Taken together, these rank cuts do not show that dropping favourites systematically improves a placement sample.

## J. Market A/E and profitability

A complete race book uses observed final decimal prices. Divide each inverse SP by the sum across verified starters. Expected wins sum those probabilities for selected runners. A/E divides fractional winner credits by expected wins. Winner and strike columns count each winning runner. For A/E and returns, divide dead-heat credit and gross payout by the number of tied winners. Started non-finishers lose one stake, and non-runners and void races do not enter the stakes. This separates prediction relative to the market from the effect of its margin. An A/E above 1 may still lose at final SP.

The rival control uses every other identified trainer in exactly the same races, with the same rank cut when shown. For the same trainer’s other comparable runners, both sides share family, country, currency, handicap flag, field band (≤8, 9–12 or 13+), quarter, class band (1–3, 4–7 or unavailable) and prize band. The comparison drops the tested class or prize dimension where applicable. Each cell receives the lower of its two counts, weighted equally within side. The own control reports equivalent stakes per side and A/E and ROI for that common support. Festival targets are compared with that trainer’s other equivalent entrants and the same festival field’s rivals. The comparisons are descriptive and keep the overlapping horse and trainer histories visible.

No additional individually priced observations in the selected patterns met the strict settlement reconciliation while failing only the complete-price-book check.

The own-runner control tempers Haggas’s 2025 valuable-handicap result because his matched comparison runners also beat the market comfortably. Mullins’s pooled festival handicap target beat his matched alternatives more clearly in both years, with A/E of 2.302 versus 0.840 and 1.687 versus 0.692 on common support. Narrower rank and meeting cells can have little overlap. Reported effective support shows how much of the original cohort can be directly compared.

## K. Cross-year stability

| Screen | Combinations | Meet reporting floor | A/E >1 in both | A/E >1 and positive after top two in both |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Named · All | 54 | 25 | 5 | 0 |
| Other trainers · All | 32 | 21 | 6 | 0 |
| Named · Favourite | 54 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| Other trainers · Favourite | 32 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| Named · Non-favourite | 54 | 18 | 3 | 0 |
| Other trainers · Non-favourite | 32 | 18 | 5 | 0 |
| Named · 2–3 | 54 | 5 | 1 | 0 |
| Other trainers · 2–3 | 32 | 1 | 1 | 0 |
| Named · 4+ | 54 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| Other trainers · 4+ | 32 | 5 | 0 | 0 |

2024 supplied the bounded screen and an earlier season of descriptive evidence. The 2025 and 2026 comparisons use fixed definitions and matching annual cutoffs. Much of 2026 already informed previous racing research and the choice of this question. Neither 2025 nor 2026 is an untouched prospective test. Common-support and same-field comparisons reduce measured race-mix confounding. Trainer decisions, unobserved entries and publication time remain unmeasured.

For all runners, 25 named placements and 21 of the other placements passed the floor. Five and six respectively exceeded A/E of 1 in both recent years. For non-favourites, 18 named and 18 other placements passed, with three and five repeating the positive A/E direction. Two rank 2–3 patterns cleared the floor and exceeded A/E of 1 in both years. Neither survived the two-race check in both. No favourites-only or rank 4+ pattern cleared the floor and repeated A/E above 1. The same horses, trainers or meetings can appear in several of these comparisons. These are counts of definitions and do not represent independent confirmations.

Valuable Haggas handicaps, pooled Mullins festival handicaps and Cromwell’s Punchestown placement have more repeatability in where horses run than in SP profitability. The conditions and single-festival subtype cuts often have too few dates or expected winners for stable year-to-year inference.

The highest-return winning races are ranked by total selected gross profit plus stakes returned to winners. Removing a race removes every stake from it. The best horse is the horse with the highest combined gross return. Meeting concentration uses the seven named festival windows, then course/date elsewhere. A named single-meeting cohort can be concentrated by construction. Each single-trainer group is also concentrated in that trainer by definition. Across the screened yards, the bounded screen counts how many different stable identities meet the same floor and repeat the direction. The 2,000 date-cluster bootstrap draws use distinct racing dates and keep same-date selections together. Repeated horses and trainers across dates and multiple comparisons limit nominal interval interpretation.

Haggas’s 2025 valuable handicap ROI stayed at +4.2% after two winning races were removed. The comparable 2026 remainder was −40.6%. The market A/E date-cluster range for the untrimmed target was roughly 1.00–2.26 in 2025 and 0.88–1.74 in 2026. The uncertainty and the 2025 comparison runners’ high A/E temper the case for outperformance.

For Mullins, the pooled festival handicaps fell to −20.8% and −60.2% after two races were removed. His Grade 1 non-favourites were positive at SP before removing the top two and negative afterwards in both years. Johnston’s non-favourite lightly raced split fell from roughly +28% and +122% to −52% and −2%. These concentration checks show how much the historical returns depended on the frequency of a few large payouts.

## M. Data gaps and Research Filters assessment

| Capability | Present evidence and boundary |
| --- | --- |
| Available now | Stable trainer/jockey IDs and cohort, course and date period, family, Jump subtype, handicap flag, race class, distance and field size, OR, prior starts, days since run, return bucket, numbered run after break and prior participant rates. |
| Market cuts | Existing starting-price conditions accept price ranges. A complete-book final-SP competition rank and joint-favourite policy are needed for direct rank cuts in this analysis. A final-SP cut is retrospective. |
| Race placement | A versioned winner-purse field with native currency, sourced handicap status and verified grade provenance would make the comparison reproducible. Exact grade and broader conditions eligibility need an independent structured classification or audited crosswalk. |
| Meeting / festival | Provider meeting IDs identify dated cards. A reviewed event calendar with course lineage, date window and year would link recurring meetings, while a separately curated race-series ID would support repeat-race targeting. |
| Trainer / horse history | Stable trainer and horse IDs are available. Prior event/course starts, prior qualifying graded race and result, prior handicap exposure, class/OR/distance moves, and coverage-aware sequence starts need derived prior-only history. Missing prior proof must remain explicit. |
| History coverage | No full pre-2024 season behind the discovery period. Sparse Irish numeric classes and verified geographical omissions limit claims. Declared entries and scratches do not establish trainer intent or executed bets. |

A data-backed research use case is a read-only historical breakdown by the seven verified meeting windows, structured handicap/prize labels and complete-market status. Carry the data lineage into the filter result. The available evidence can also support a separate prior-only graded-to-handicap and lightly raced report once the grade and history coverage gates are explicit. [Existing Research Filter schema](../src/lib/racing/research-rule.ts)

For a graded-to-handicap comparison the most important gaps are independent Grade/Group classification, the full earlier starter history and proof of whether gaps are genuine. For named festivals, exact date-and-course scopes are usable for the seven reviewed meetings. Season-wide comparisons need wider race metadata coverage. A saved filter cannot currently reproduce all conditions and completeness gates in this report.

## N. Hypotheses for subsequent independent testing

1. **Haggas in valuable Flat handicaps.** Freeze trainer ID, AW/Turf, structured handicap status, winner’s first prize of at least 25,000 in recorded GBP or EUR, and the complete-book evidence gates. On a new observation window, test whether his market A/E exceeds one and exceeds his comparable ordinary-handicap A/E under the same common-support weighting.

2. **Mullins Grade 1 non-favourites.** Freeze trainer ID, non-handicap Chase/Hurdle, the identity-matched Grade 1 designation with corroborating Class 1, and complete-book competition ranks above one. Test whether A/E exceeds one and his matched non-favourite counterparts in other non-handicap jumps races. Freeze the grade coverage boundary before observation.

3. **Cromwell’s Punchestown placement.** Freeze trainer and Punchestown course identity and all complete-book Chase/Hurdle starts across dates. Test excess A/E against the final-SP book and his common-support starters elsewhere. Treat the existing SP losses as part of the hypothesis record.

For these follow-ups use distinct dates and horses as coverage checks. Each test needs a new, dated observation period fixed before its first eligible race. Fix the end date and minimum expected-winner and date coverage in advance. Report A/E with date-cluster intervals, exact-field rivals and the fixed largest-race sensitivity. Keep ROI secondary and record the result even if weak or inconclusive. A final-SP rank test describes market calibration at the off.

### Detailed original populations and conditional controls

Every cell for the target gives stakes · winners · strike rate · mean/median SP · market E · A/E · £1 P/L (ROI) · dates/horses. The control columns give common-support effective stakes, same-trainer target → other A/E and ROI, then exact-race rival n/A/E/ROI. A dash indicates unavailable evidence. Control populations and targeted rows are not pooled.

### Verification

The read-only repeatable-read PostgreSQL export joined `races`, `race_runners`, `courses`, `horses`, `trainers` and Sporting Life `source_imports` full-result payloads by provider race and ride identifiers. The protocol was fixed before outcome aggregation. Verified input examples include local Cheltenham, Aintree, Punchestown, Royal Ascot and repaired Newmarket payloads. Across each complete sample, normalised probabilities and canonical winner credits reconcile to one per race. This research did not modify historical records, operational collection, saved rules, ratings, Tissue models or frozen forecasts.

Protocol SHA-256 c07093ef4dcf706316efbcd10a17eb201028b2682e8df8d742202ae020ff73d3. Database projection SHA-256 698f7a72bcde98d4560264aa4d0a09f04c6ba4394ab7649d49c69fa70a981227. 99 meeting index reads were pinned by content hash, comprising 84 distinct files. The focused existing suite passed 118 tests for settlement, Research Filter and trainer history behaviour and market analysis. The one-off TypeScript analysis passed strict type checking and ESLint.
